### 0037 · CHATGPT · 2026-10-10T13:33:16Z

## 10/10/2026 14:32 | Business Goal → Acquisition + Retention

Partner, this section actually connects directly to the funnel we were just discussing.

### What the trainer says

He asks:

> **“What is the main goal of business?”**

Participants answer profit, customer satisfaction, sustainability, etc.

His answer is:

> **“Making a new customer and sustaining the existing customer.”**

He argues that **profit is the result**, rather than the primary operating objective.

His model is essentially:

```text
TARGET AUDIENCE
      ↓
MARKETING
      ↓
NEW CUSTOMER ACQUISITION
      ↓
CUSTOMER
      ↓
Trust / Relationship / Value
      ↓
RETENTION
      ↓
REPEAT PURCHASE
      ↓
PROFIT
```

That is much clearer than his earlier broad statement that marketing is everything.

---

### 🟢 The acquisition-vs-retention distinction is valuable

He gives the ₹100 example:

> Spend ₹100 acquiring someone once.  
> If that customer returns repeatedly without needing the same acquisition spend, subsequent purchases become economically more attractive.

This is pointing toward two very important commercial metrics that he hasn't named yet:

**CAC — Customer Acquisition Cost**

> How much did it cost us to acquire the customer?

and

**LTV/CLV — Customer Lifetime Value**

> How much economic value does that customer generate over the relationship?

So the mature version of what he's teaching is:

```text
Acquire customer
      ↓
CAC
      ↓
Deliver value
      ↓
Retention
      ↓
Repeat / Expand
      ↓
LTV
```

A business where **LTV comfortably exceeds CAC** has a much better chance of having sustainable unit economics.

---

### One correction to the trainer

I wouldn't state:

> “The main goal of every business is acquiring and retaining customers.”

as a universal definition.

A stronger formulation is:

> **A sustainable commercial business creates value for customers and captures enough of that value to survive and generate an acceptable return.**

Acquisition and retention are mechanisms within that.

Otherwise, we could theoretically acquire 1 million customers while losing £10 on every customer. That's not a healthy business.

Think:

**Customer value + Acquisition + Retention + Unit economics → Sustainable business.**

---

## 🔥 This also answers your question from 14:30

We can now connect all the terminology cleanly:

```text
                     MARKET
                       ↓
                TARGET AUDIENCE
                       ↓
        ┌──────── MARKETING ────────┐
        │                           │
 Advertising                   Campaigns
 Content / SEO                 Events etc.
        │                           │
        └─────────────┬─────────────┘
                      ↓
                    LEAD
                      ↓
                     MQL
                      ↓
                     SQL
                      ↓
                  PROSPECT
                      ↓
                OPPORTUNITY
                      ↓
             ───── SALES ─────
                      ↓
                  CUSTOMER
                      ↓
          ONBOARDING / DELIVERY
                      ↓
               CUSTOMER VALUE
                      ↓
          CUSTOMER SUCCESS / CX
                      ↓
                 RETENTION
                      ↓
          RENEWAL / REPEAT SALE
                      ↓
              UPSELL / EXPANSION
                      ↓
                 ADVOCACY
                      ↓
                  REFERRAL
                      ↓
                 NEW LEADS
```

**CRM can sit across nearly this entire journey.**

That's why CRM isn't merely a “lead database.”

---

### 🟡 One more distinction from today's teaching

The trainer repeatedly attributes repeat purchasing to **trust**.

Trust matters, but:

> **Trust gets me willing to buy again. Value gives me a reason to buy again.**

For SaaS especially:

**Trust without value → churn.**

A customer can completely trust us as people and still cancel because the product doesn't generate enough value.

So for our products I'd use:

> **Acquire → Activate → Deliver measurable value → Build trust → Retain → Expand.**

That's stronger than simply **Acquire → Build trust → Repeat sale**.

---

### Next: “Sell me this pen”

He's now entering the classic **“art of selling a pen”** exercise.

This should be interesting because it will reveal whether he teaches sales as **persuasion**, or properly moves toward **need discovery → problem → value → qualification → solution**.

Let's capture what he teaches before judging it.

## 10/10/2026 14:34 | Tea-Shop Dilemma — Your Answer

Partner, **your answer is a strong answer**:

> **The tea shop that provides the better service / customer experience will make more profit.**

Given his setup:

- same milk
- same tea powder
- same taste
- presumably similar product

then the differentiation has to come from something **outside the core tea itself**.

That could be:

**service + cleanliness + speed + friendliness + seating + convenience + relationship + ambience + remembering the customer + consistency = Customer Experience (CX).**

### But I suspect the trainer may be heading somewhere slightly different

Because immediately before this he said:

> “We have to play with the mindset of people.”

So his expected answer may be something involving **perceived value / customer psychology**, rather than simply better service.

For example, two identical tea shops:

```text
SHOP A                         SHOP B

Same tea                      Same tea
Same ingredients              Same ingredients
Same price                    Same price

Ordinary experience           Owner knows your name
                              Faster service
                              Better presentation
                              Friendly conversation
                              Comfortable environment
                              "Your usual tea, sir?"
       ↓                            ↓
 Commodity                     Experience / relationship
```

Shop B hasn't necessarily created a better **tea**.

It has created a better **reason to choose it**.

### This connects nicely with what we just discussed

Remember:

**Marketing gets me there.**  
**Sales gets me to buy.**  
**Product gives me the tea.**  
**Customer experience makes me want to return.**  
**Retention makes me economically valuable over time.**

And therefore:

> **Advertising may acquire the first visit. Customer experience can acquire the second visit without another advertisement.**

That actually supports the trainer's previous CAC/retention argument very well.

Let's see what **his answer to the tea-shop dilemma** is. Don't change your answer yet — **“better service/customer experience” is defensible**, and comparing it with his intended answer will tell us something useful.

### 0043 · CHATGPT · 2026-10-10T13:37:50Z

## 10/10/2026 14:37 | Previous Transcript — What Trainer Taught

Yes, partner — I misunderstood your question. You meant **explain the trainer's teaching in the large transcript immediately before the tea-shop transcript**, not connect it to an older class.

Here is what he actually taught in that section.

### 1. He asks: **What is the main goal of business?**

Students answered things such as:

- Making money/profit
- Customer satisfaction
- Sustaining in the market

The trainer **rejects profit as the primary goal**.

His position is:

> **The main goal of business is to acquire new customers and sustain/retain existing customers.**

His causal argument is:

```text
Fulfil customer requirements
        ↓
Acquire customers
        ↓
Build relationship / rapport
        ↓
Retain those customers
        ↓
Repeat business
        ↓
Profit
```

So he is treating **profit as an outcome of customer acquisition + retention**, rather than the first objective.

---

### 2. His small-clothing-shop example is about **relationship**

This was an important part of his argument.

He compares:

- a large textile store,
- a small textile shop,
- another medium-sized shop.

All of them can survive and make profit.

His explanation is essentially:

> Each has its own **fixed/set of customers**.

The small shop owner may have a customer thinking:

> “Whenever I need clothes, I buy from this particular shop.”

According to the trainer, the shop owner achieves that by developing **rapport and relationship** with that customer.

Therefore:

**Relationship → repeat customer → continuing revenue/profit.**

---

### 3. Then he introduces what we would call **Customer Acquisition Cost**

He says acquiring a new customer is expensive.

His simplified example:

```text
Spend ₹100 advertising
        ↓
Acquire customer
        ↓
Customer buys
```

But if you retain that person, they might buy again:

```text
1st purchase     ₹100
2nd purchase     ₹100
3rd purchase     ₹100
4th purchase     ₹100
                   ↑
       without repeatedly spending
       the same advertising cost
```

He even extends the hypothetical to the customer later spending ₹1,000 or ₹10,000.

He doesn't use the formal terms in this transcript, but conceptually he's teaching:

**CAC — Customer Acquisition Cost**  
and  
**LTV/CLV — Customer Lifetime Value.**

That's a meaningful business concept.

---

### 4. He attributes the repeat purchase primarily to **trust**

His statement is effectively:

> You advertise once to acquire them; when they repeatedly return, the additional revenue comes because you built trust.

So his model becomes:

**Advertisement → Acquisition → Trust → Retention → Repeat purchase → Profit**

This connects directly to his earlier **Awareness → Trust** discussion.

---

### 5. Apple is his example of retention

He then uses Apple:

> Existing users move to newer/more expensive devices, and this repeat purchasing contributes to profit.

He uses an expensive/foldable-phone example to say something else important:

> Whether an expensive product is “necessary” depends on the customer's requirement.

So underneath the Apple story, there are actually **two concepts**:

**Retention:** existing customers buy again.

**Customer-specific value:** what seems unnecessary to one person may satisfy another person's need/desire.

---

# What is genuinely useful here?

There is a solid commercial lesson underneath his simplified explanation:

> **Don't evaluate a business only by whether you can make the first sale. Ask whether the customer receives enough value to stay, return, renew or buy more.**

For **our money-making products**, that is very important.

We shouldn't only ask:

> “Can RevRescue get a customer?”

We should ask:

> **Why would that customer still pay in month 3, month 6 and month 12?**

That brings us to:

**CAC → Activation → Value → Retention → LTV → Profitability.**

### One place I'd correct him

I wouldn't accept **“acquiring and retaining customers is the main goal of every business”** as literally complete.

You can acquire and retain customers and **still lose money**.

For example:

```text
Cost to acquire customer = £500
Cost to serve customer   = £1,000
Customer lifetime revenue = £700

Excellent retention perhaps...
but economically bad business.
```

So the stronger business model is:

> **Create customer value → acquire customers economically → deliver the value → retain appropriate customers → capture sufficient value/profit → sustain the business.**

That is what the trainer's previous transcript was getting at, but his version simplifies the economics considerably.

இமேஜின். த ப்ராஃபிட் இஸ் கால்குலேட்டட் ஆஃப்டர் 30 டேஸ். எண்ட் ஆப். எவ்ரி மந்த். சோ, ஹூ மேக்ஸ் தி ப்ராஃபிட் நவ்? காஸ்ட் பர் கப். நார்மல் தான். இன்னைக்கு சென்னையில போய் ஒரு டீ குடிச்சாங்கன்னா ₹15. ஓகே, ₹15 சேம். காஸ்ட். வந்து ரெண்டு டீ ஷாப்லயும் சேம் தான். கமான், கஸ்டமர் சாட்டிஸ்பேக்ஷன் மேட்டர்ஸ். ஓகே, சோ நிறைய பேத்துக்கு வந்து ஒரு சில பேத்துக்கு டீ ஷாப் A ல இருக்கற ஆன்சர் சில. பேர் பார்த்தீங்க அப்படின்னா A ல இருக்கற டீ ஷாப் ஓனர் வந்து நல்ல ரிலேஷன்ஷிப் வச்சிருப்பாரு. அதனால வந்து ஐ வில் கோ வித் A அப்படின்னு போயிடுவாங்க. ஆனா இன்னும் சில பேர் வந்து எனக்கு B தான் புடிச்சிருக்கு அப்படின்னு சொல்லி B க்கு போவாங்கல. சோ அந்த மாதிரி பாக்குறப்ப, ரெண்டு இதுலயுமே மோர் ஆர் லெஸ் சாட்டிஸ்பேக்ஷன் இருக்கும் தானே? ஹூ டஸ் மார்க்கெட்டிங் பார் ஷாப்? யா, ஐ அம் டூயிங் மார்க்கெட்டிங் பார் தி ஷாப். யா, ரெண்டு பேருமே பெரிய. எல்லாம் கிடையாது. சின்ன சின்னதா டீ ஷாப் சென்னையில எல்லாம் பார்த்திருப்பீங்க. வெரி பெரிய சிட்டிஸ் எல்லாம் அந்த மாதிரி ஒரு ரெண்டு டீ ஷாப் ஒரே மாதிரி இருக்கு. கஸ்டமர் சர்வீஸ் அண்ட் காஸ்ட் கண்ட்ரோல். ஓகே, கஸ்டமர் சர்வீஸ் பண்றத பொறுத்து, இந்த டீ ஷாப் விச் கிவ்ஸ் குவாலிட்டி அண்ட் ட்ரஸ்ட். ஓகே, சோ. குவாலிட்டி குடுக்குறவங்க, ட்ரஸ்ட் குடுக்குறவங்க. ஓகே, அதான் நான் சொன்னேன், ரெண்டு டீ ஷாப்லயும் டேஸ்ட் ஒரே மாதிரி தான் இருக்கும். ரெண்டு டீ ஷாப்லயும் ஒரே மாதிரி மில்க் தான் யூஸ் பண்றாங்க. ஓகே, ஒரே மாதிரி கிளாஸ்ல தான் டீ குடுக்குறாங்க. எல்லாமே ஒரே மாதிரி தான் இருக்கும். பட் அட் தி எண்ட், சம் ஒன் ஹாஸ் டு மேக் ப்ராஃபிட். அட் தி எண்ட் ஆப் 30 டேஸ், சம் ஒன் ஹாஸ் டு மேக் ப்ராஃபிட். ஆம் ஐ ரைட்? சோ, ஹூ மேக்ஸ் தி ப்ராஃபிட்? கஸ்டமர் சிஆர்எம், கஸ்டமர் ரிலேஷன்ஷிப் மேனேஜர். ஓகே, ஓகே. அட்வெர்டைஸ்மென்ட் ஒன்னு டஸ் அட்வெர்டைஸ்மென்ட். ஓகே, சோ. ஐ கேன் 100% அஸ்யூர் யூ, நீங்க இந்த கொஸ்டின்க்கு மட்டும் ஆன்சர் பண்ணிட்டீங்க அப்படின்னா, யூ கேன் அச்சீவ். நீங்க வந்து எந்த ப்ராடக்ட்டு வேணாலும் ஈஸியா சேல் பண்ண முடியும். ஓகே, நீங்க A யூஸ் பண்ணி இன்னும் ஈஸியா உங்களால பண்ண முடியும். இந்த ஒரு குவாலிட்டி மட்டும் உங்ககிட்ட இருந்துச்சு. இந்த ஒரு, இந்த ஒரு குவாலிட்டி மட்டும் ஈஸியா அப்பாயிண்ட் பண்ணிட்டீங்க அப்படின்னா, சோ. மெயின் டு சர்வ் தி கஸ்டமர் வித் பொலைட்னஸ் அண்ட் கைண்ட்னஸ். ஓகே, கஸ்டமர் நம்ம எந்த அளவுக்கு ட்ரீட் பண்றோமோ, அதுக்கு ஏத்த மாதிரி ப்ராஃபிட் வரும். ஓகே, அண்டர்ஸ்டாண்ட்? ஹூ ஹாஸ் லெஸ் எக்ஸ்பென்ஸ்? ரெண்டுமே. மோர் ஆர் லெஸ் எக்ஸ்பென்ஸ் சேமாதான் இருக்கும். இவரும் வந்து மாசம் ₹10000க்கு ரெண்டு குடுக்குறாரு. இவரும் மாசம் ₹10000 ரெண்டு குடுக்குறாரு. டீ ஷாப்புக்கு. சோ, எக்ஸ்பென்ஸ் மோர் ஆர் லெஸ் சேம். யா, சோ. பட் அட் தி எண்ட் ஆப் 30 டேஸ், சம் ஒன் ஹாஸ் டு மேக் ப்ராஃபிட், ரைட்? ## 10/10/2026 14:42 | Tea-Shop Dilemma — Still Unresolved

Partner, now the trainer has **tightened the thought experiment considerably**. Importantly, **he has not revealed his answer yet**, so we shouldn't invent it.

### What he has deliberately eliminated

He is saying assume A and B are almost identical:

| Variable | Shop A | Shop B |
|---|---|---|
| Tea ingredients | Same | Same |
| Taste | Same | Same |
| Glass/presentation | Same | Same |
| Tea price | ₹15 | ₹15 |
| Rent/expense | More or less same | More or less same |
| Customer numbers | Vary by day | Vary by day |
| 30-day comparison | Required | Required |

Students suggested **customer satisfaction, service, CRM, trust, cleanliness, cost control, consistency, advertising, snacks, reviews, marketing, etc.**

He keeps eliminating or neutralising these answers.

That means my earlier answer **“better customer experience” is not the answer he is looking for**. It is a valid real-world differentiator, but his puzzle is designed around another principle.

### The interesting clue he has now given

He suddenly changes from tea shops to **pairs of major competitors**:

**Football:** Messi ↔ Ronaldo  
**Bollywood:** Shah Rukh Khan ↔ Salman Khan  
**Indian cars:** Tata ↔ Mahindra

And specifically:

> “Who is Tata's competitor? Mahindra. Who is Mahindra's competitor? Tata.”

That's a very strong clue.

He appears to be moving toward a principle involving **competition itself** — perhaps competitors, differentiation, relative positioning, or how one benefits/responds because the other exists.

But I don't want to jump to the answer yet because **he hasn't completed the analogy**.

### One thing in his puzzle is logically problematic

He repeatedly says:

> “At the end of 30 days, somebody HAS to make more profit. They cannot have exactly equal profit; even ₹1 difference will exist.”

Mathematically, that's **not necessarily true**.

If every relevant variable is truly identical, equal profit is entirely possible. And if some unknown variable differs, we cannot determine whether **A or B** makes more profit without knowing that variable.

So there are actually two layers:

**Real business reasoning:** insufficient information to determine A or B.

**Trainer's exercise:** he has a particular hidden variable/concept that he wants students to discover.

For the class, we should follow the second layer and find out what concept he's teaching.

### Why this section is useful for us

Notice what he's doing pedagogically:

> **Product is the same → price is the same → costs are roughly the same → customers roughly balance → what invisible factor creates the difference?**

That is actually a valuable question for our own products.

Because if 10 companies can all build essentially the same AI product, **why does one make money and the other nine don't?**

That is directly connected to our **40+ builds / zero-revenue problem**.

And now his **Tata ↔ Mahindra / Messi ↔ Ronaldo** clue suggests he's about to introduce the missing factor.

**Let's capture his next 2–3 minutes exactly before deciding what his intended answer is.**

## 10/10/2026 14:44 | Trainer's “Top Two” Clue

Now his intended direction is much clearer, partner.

### What the trainer is claiming

He's building a pattern:

| Field | His “Top Two” |
|---|---|
| Football | Messi ↔ Ronaldo |
| Bollywood | Shah Rukh Khan ↔ Salman Khan |
| Indian cars | Tata ↔ Mahindra |
| Tamil cinema earlier | Rajinikanth ↔ Kamal Haasan |
| Tamil cinema later | Vijay ↔ Ajith |
| Mobile phones | Apple ↔ Samsung |
| Next example | Oppo ↔ Vivo |

Then his question is:

> **“Why does every industry have two people/companies at the top? Why not 3, 4 or 5?”**

So this is no longer really a **tea profitability calculation**. He's constructing a lesson about **competition/rivalry and human perception**.

### I think I can see where he's heading — but let's not lock it yet

The likely idea is something like:

> **A strong competitor helps create/strengthen the other competitor.**

For example:

```text
Apple  ← competition → Samsung
Messi  ← rivalry → Ronaldo
Vijay  ← rivalry → Ajith
```

Potentially his tea-shop answer could become:

> **Both shops benefit because the competing shop creates comparison/choice/market attention.**

But **that's still my inference**, not his answer. Let's wait for him to explicitly connect the “top two” phenomenon back to Tea Shop A/B.

### ⚠️ His factual premise is weak

His statement:

> “In every industry only two people are on top.”

is **not a general business law**.

Many markets have one dominant player, three or more major competitors, fragmented competition, regional leaders, or rapidly changing rankings. Even some of his examples depend heavily on geography, time period, metric and personal perception.

So record this as:

> **TRAINER FRAMEWORK/CLAIM: markets and public perception tend to form two dominant rivals. Source/evidence not established.**

There *are* legitimate concepts he may eventually be approaching—**duopoly, competitive positioning, differentiation, category framing, rivalry increasing salience**—but we shouldn't retrofit those theories onto him until he explains his conclusion.

And this is now quite different from your original answer **“better customer experience.”** Let's see whether he can logically connect his competitor-pair idea back to which tea shop earns more profit.

## 10/10/2026 14:47 | Trainer Is Teaching Comparison

Partner, now his intended concept is becoming much clearer.

### What he is saying now

He is **rejecting “the best product wins.”**

His examples are deliberately making that point:

- Tata/Mahindra being prominent doesn't prove they make objectively the “best” cars.
- He brings up Volvo to challenge a simple “best safety/product wins” explanation.
- Vijay/Ajith being perceived as the major pair doesn't mean Vijay Sethupathi or other actors are inferior.
- Maruti Suzuki may have more users, yet people can still frame a particular comparison around Tata vs Mahindra.

His emerging argument is:

> **Human beings simplify choices by comparing prominent alternatives.**

And the line from the participant that he liked was:

> **“People always compare those top two and choose one of them.”**

He said this was **“almost” the answer**.

---

## Oppo–Vivo is his bridge back to the tea shops

His claim is:

> Oppo and Vivo are under the same owner/company, were launched together in India, but were presented as two different products/brands.

Then he asks:

> Why would somebody create **two alternatives instead of one?**

And connects that to:

```text
Tea Shop A  ↔  Tea Shop B

Oppo        ↔  Vivo

Tata        ↔  Mahindra

Apple       ↔  Samsung

Vijay       ↔  Ajith
```

So he's moving toward **comparison creating choice/attention**.

### But there are two separate questions getting mixed together

This is important.

**Question 1: Why do customers compare two alternatives?**

That's about **choice psychology / positioning / perception**.

**Question 2: Which tea shop makes more profit after 30 days?**

That's about **revenue minus costs**.

The first does **not mathematically answer the second**.

He's deliberately constructing a puzzle to teach a psychological/marketing concept rather than doing an actual profit calculation.

---

## His Oppo/Vivo factual claims need verification

He's making several specific factual claims:

> “Oppo and Vivo are the same company.”  
> “They have the same owner.”  
> “They launched in India on the same day/date.”

We should mark these:

**🔴 TRAINER CLAIM — not yet verified.**

There is a known historical corporate relationship around these brands, but **“same company/same owner” is a much stronger statement**, and the same-day India launch claim is very specific.

Don't put those into our learning system as facts until verified.

---

## Most important learning so far

His useful underlying idea appears to be:

**Customers don't necessarily evaluate every available option objectively.**

Instead:

```text
Huge number of possible choices
            ↓
A few become mentally prominent
            ↓
Customer creates a consideration set
            ↓
Compares alternatives
            ↓
Perceived differentiation
            ↓
Choice
```

That's commercially important for us.

Our problem may therefore not merely be:

> “Is our product better?”

It can be:

> **“When the customer thinks about solving this problem, are we even inside the 2–3 products they consider?”**

That is **positioning/distribution**, not product engineering.

He's still withholding the final answer to his tea-shop puzzle. **I would wait**, because the next part should reveal exactly what he calls this principle.

We should **not try to prove him wrong by throwing out a clever guess**. 😄

This is actually a good opportunity for the experiment you've been asking for: **independent reasoning rather than answer generation.**

We have until tomorrow to model the problem.

### First observation: as stated, the puzzle has no deterministic answer

If A and B genuinely have identical:

**price + product + quality + costs + customer volume + service + conditions**

then there isn't enough information to determine whether A or B has higher profit.

Formally:

**Profit = Revenue − Cost**

If all revenue and cost determinants are identical:

**Profit A = Profit B.**

His insistence that “there must at least be ₹1 difference” introduces an **unstated variable**.

So the real puzzle is:

> **What hidden variable does the trainer want us to discover?**

That's a much better question than “A or B?”

### And his clues narrow that hidden variable

He has repeatedly constructed **pairs**:

```text

Apple       ↔ Samsung
```

> Why two?  
> Why do people compare them?  
> Why not three/four/five?  
> Fans are customers.

So **my current hypothesis**, not the trainer's answer, is that the hidden concept concerns **relative positioning / rivalry / customer identity / polarisation or choice architecture**, rather than tea quality itself.

But we should leave that as a hypothesis tonight.

### One especially important thing for our course evaluation

His statement **“AI cannot answer this”** gives us a testable claim.

We now have the exact puzzle and all the constraints **before tomorrow's reveal**.

### 0053 · CHATGPT · 2026-10-10T13:50:58Z

## 10/10/2026 14:50 | New Clue — Competitor Proximity

Partner, this adds an important clue. The trainer still **has not revealed the answer**, but he has narrowed it further.

### What he added

He gives a physical-market example:

> Saravana Stores is already in Chromepet.  
> Pothys opens opposite it.  
> Does that mean Saravana Stores stops making profit? **No.**  
> Both can make profit.

Then he returns to:

> **“But who makes MORE profit?”**

And again says the class answers—customer satisfaction, both businesses grow, promotion, trends, etc.—are reasonable but **not the exact answer he wants**.

### His argument is becoming more specific

He's challenging this simple assumption:

```text
Competitor arrives
      ↓
My customers are divided
      ↓
My profit necessarily falls
```

Instead, he's suggesting something closer to:

```text
Competitor A  ←→  Competitor B
        ↓
Comparison / attention
        ↓
More people attracted to that market/category/location
        ↓
Both businesses can benefit
```

That would explain why he deliberately chose **Pothys opposite Saravana Stores**, rather than two unrelated stores miles apart.

There is a real business concept adjacent to this: **agglomeration / clustering**—competitors sometimes locate near one another because a cluster can attract more shoppers and make the location itself a destination. But **the trainer hasn't used that term**, so I wouldn't say that's his answer yet.

### His AI claim is now stronger too

He's saying this answer comes from:

> **“Originality of human beings. AI can never ever replace [it].”**

That's a separate claim from the tea-shop puzzle.

I disagree with the absolute form **“AI can never answer this type of question.”** AI can reason about competition, clustering, positioning, consumer psychology and hidden-variable puzzles.

But there's a deeper point where I partly agree with him:

> **Human lived experience, motivations and context shouldn't simply be treated as replaceable by generated patterns.**

That's very different from saying AI cannot solve his particular puzzle.

### Current checkpoint

We now have several clues:

**two competitors → comparison → fan/customer groups → both can profit → competitor can open directly opposite another → both can continue growing → nevertheless one eventually has slightly more profit.**

I still would **not commit to his final answer**.

And that's useful. Tomorrow, when he reveals his “exact answer,” we can test whether it actually follows from these clues—or whether the puzzle was constructed backwards around the answer he wanted to teach.

## 10/10/2026 14:57 | AIBGM → BMC Starts

Partner, **this is the point where the ₹14,000 course may start becoming materially useful for us.** He's moved from general marketing concepts into his actual framework.

### What the trainer says AIBGM is

His definition is essentially:

> **AIBGM = Generative AI + Business Model Canvas (BMC)**

He says use tools such as ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, Grok, DeepSeek, etc., together with the **Business Model Canvas** to create/improve a business model.

His promised outcome is:

> Use AI to become more **prudent, productive and scalable**, and learn advanced prompting/business tactics through the nine components.

So his framework appears to be:

```text
Traditional BMC
     +
Generative AI
     ↓
AI-BGM
     ↓
9 business-model areas
     ↓
Prompt AI separately for each
     ↓
Business tactics / plan / improvements
```

### Important terminology correction

He says BMC was developed by **“Alexander Oswald.”**

The name is **Alexander Osterwalder**, who developed the Business Model Canvas with work involving Yves Pigneur and others and popularized it through *Business Model Generation*.

Also, the standard BMC calls these **nine building blocks**, not normally “nine principles.”

That distinction matters because I expect the nine things he's about to show are the familiar BMC components:

**Customer Segments, Value Propositions, Channels, Customer Relationships, Revenue Streams, Key Resources, Key Activities, Key Partnerships, Cost Structure.**

We'll compare his slide when he presents it rather than assume.

---

## 🟢 His strongest idea here

The interesting part isn't simply:

> “Ask AI to create a business plan.”

We've already done far more sophisticated things than that.

The potentially valuable part is **forcing AI through a complete business-model structure** rather than asking a vague question such as:

> “How can I make money from this product?”

For one of our products, that forces nine different questions:

```text
Who exactly buys it?
        ↓
What value do they receive?
        ↓
How do they discover/buy it?
        ↓
What relationship is required?
        ↓
How exactly does money enter?
        ↓
What resources are necessary?
        ↓
What activities must happen?
        ↓
Who/what must we depend on?
        ↓
What does all of this cost?
```

This is highly relevant to our portfolio because **we've historically over-weighted product/build capability**.

A technically excellent product can still have weak answers to:

**Customer Segment + Channels + Customer Relationship + Revenue Model.**

That is precisely where a money-run product can fail.

---

## 🔴 Where I disagree strongly with him

Trainer says that a human consultant with 10–20 years' experience cannot give a fully prudent/productive answer because humans have limited knowledge, whereas AI has vast data/understanding.

That's overstated.

AI has **breadth**, but that doesn't automatically produce:

> correct market knowledge + tacit experience + customer evidence + judgment + current facts.

A 20-year domain expert may know something extremely important that isn't written anywhere AI has access to.

And AI can confidently generate a beautifully structured BMC that is **commercially wrong**.

For us the stronger model is:

> **Human/domain evidence + AI breadth/simulation + structured framework + market evidence + experiment → better decision.**

Not:

> **AI knows more → therefore AI gives the better business answer.**

That distinction is particularly important given our own experience: **40+ things can be built with AI; that doesn't prove customers will pay for them.**

---

## One sentence he said that I would reverse

He effectively says:

> “AI can act as doctor, policeman, director, editor, writer—it depends whom I ask AI to act as.”

That's classic **role prompting**.

It can influence response framing, but:

> **“Act as a 20-year business consultant” does not give the model 20 years of lived business experience.**

So if his “advanced prompting” mainly becomes **“Act as an expert + BMC question”**, that will be relatively basic for us.

If instead he teaches **evidence → assumptions → BMC → competing strategies → validation → metrics → iteration**, that could become valuable.

### Course checkpoint

So far:

**AIBGM itself does not appear to be a new underlying business framework.** From his explanation, it is his method of applying generative AI to the established **Business Model Canvas**.

The next part—the **nine blocks and exactly how he uses AI against each one**—is what we should evaluate carefully. That's where we'll find out whether AIBGM is merely *BMC + prompts* or whether he has actually added a useful methodology on top of it.

## 10/10/2026 14:59 | Business Model Generation — Our Baseline

Yes, partner. **Good experiment.** From this point I will build an independent baseline **without using the trainer's AIBGM material and without using your stored memory as the source for the model**. I checked external sources about the original book/framework, and I also checked the GitHub repositories currently visible to me.

## 1. What exactly is *Business Model Generation*?

*Business Model Generation: A Handbook for Visionaries, Game Changers, and Challengers* is a 2010 book by **Alexander Osterwalder and Yves Pigneur**, published by Wiley. It is not merely a book explaining a nine-box diagram. It presents a broader method for **describing, designing, challenging and innovating business models**. citeturn0search4turn0search14

The core definition is:

> A business model explains how an organization **creates, delivers and captures value**. citeturn0search0

That three-part formulation is important:

```text
CREATE VALUE
What valuable thing are we creating, and for whom?

       ↓

DELIVER VALUE
How does that value actually reach the customer?

       ↓

CAPTURE VALUE
How does the business make enough money/value from doing it?
```

So it is **not the same thing as a business plan**.

A business plan might contain forecasts, hiring plans, detailed market analysis, execution plans, etc.

The Canvas is more like the **architecture of the business itself**.

---

# 2. The book is much bigger than the 9 boxes

This is important for evaluating the course.

The book's structure goes roughly:

**Canvas → Patterns → Design → Strategy → Process.** citeturn0search3turn0search13

### Canvas

This introduces the nine building blocks and gives you a common visual language for representing a business.

### Patterns

It then examines recurring business-model structures, including:

- unbundled businesses
- Long Tail
- multi-sided platforms
- FREE/freemium-type models
- open business models. citeturn0search3

This is much more interesting than simply filling nine boxes.

For example, a marketplace has fundamentally different economics from ordinary SaaS because it may need to create value for **two different customer groups simultaneously**.

### Design

The book then moves into methods for actually inventing models:

- customer insights
- ideation
- visual thinking
- prototyping
- storytelling
- scenarios. citeturn0search13

This is important: **you aren't supposed to fill one Canvas and declare it correct.**

You can prototype several competing business models.

### Strategy

Then it considers the business environment, evaluating models, Blue Ocean Strategy perspectives and managing multiple business models. citeturn0search9turn0search13

### Process

Finally, it addresses the process of designing business models. citeturn0search13

So if the course teaches only:

> Nine boxes → ask AI nine prompts → finished business model

then it is using **only one portion of the underlying methodology**.

We'll see.

---

# 3. The original nine Business Model Canvas blocks

The canonical nine are: citeturn0search0turn0search2

| # | Block | Fundamental question |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Customer Segments** | Who exactly are we serving? |
| 2 | **Value Propositions** | Why should they choose us? |
| 3 | **Channels** | How do they discover, buy and receive it? |
| 4 | **Customer Relationships** | What relationship do we establish/maintain? |
| 5 | **Revenue Streams** | How does money enter? |
| 6 | **Key Resources** | What assets/capabilities must exist? |
| 7 | **Key Activities** | What must we actually do well? |
| 8 | **Key Partnerships** | Who/what outside us is essential? |
| 9 | **Cost Structure** | What does the model cost to operate? |

And they are **interdependent**. Change the customer segment and you may need different channels, value proposition, activities and costs. Strategyzer explicitly warns that a filled canvas is not itself a decision and recommends treating it as a claim to be challenged and revised as evidence arrives. citeturn0search2

That last point is critical.

---

# 4. I'll create our own AI-era business-model method

I would **not** simply tell an LLM:

> “Fill my Business Model Canvas.”

That can produce nine beautifully written hallucinations.

Instead, my independent model would be:

# **EVIDENCE → MODEL → ECONOMICS → TEST → LEARN**

with the BMC inside it.

```text
              ┌─────────────────────┐
              │  1. REAL PROBLEM    │
              │  + EVIDENCE         │
              └──────────┬──────────┘
                         ↓
              ┌─────────────────────┐
              │ 2. CUSTOMER / JOB   │
              └──────────┬──────────┘
                         ↓
              ┌─────────────────────┐
              │ 3. VALUE / OUTCOME  │
              └──────────┬──────────┘
                         ↓
              ┌─────────────────────┐
              │ 4. BUSINESS MODEL   │
              │    (9 BMC blocks)   │
              └──────────┬──────────┘
                         ↓
              ┌─────────────────────┐
              │ 5. UNIT ECONOMICS   │
              │ CAC / LTV / Margin  │
              └──────────┬──────────┘
                         ↓
              ┌─────────────────────┐
              │ 6. DISTRIBUTION     │
              │ Can buyers find us? │
              └──────────┬──────────┘
                         ↓
              ┌─────────────────────┐
              │ 7. ASSUMPTIONS      │
              │ Fact vs hypothesis  │
              └──────────┬──────────┘
                         ↓
              ┌─────────────────────┐
              │ 8. TEST             │
              │ Cheapest evidence   │
              └──────────┬──────────┘
                         ↓
              ┌─────────────────────┐
              │ 9. OUTCOME/METRIC   │
              └──────────┬──────────┘
                         ↓
                   LEARN / CHANGE
                         │
                         └──────→ repeat
```

That is **my baseline**, independently of what he is about to teach.

---

# 5. Our AI should NOT answer the Canvas directly

This is the biggest change I would make for the AI era.

For every Canvas block, AI should produce **four fields**, not one answer:

```text
CLAIM
What we currently believe

EVIDENCE
What actually supports it

CONFIDENCE
High / Medium / Low

TEST
What would prove/disprove it cheaply
```

Example:

### Customer Segment

**Claim**

> Independent dental clinics with 3–15 practitioners are our best initial customer.

**Evidence**

> None yet.

**Confidence**

> Low.

**Test**

> Compare search demand, competitor reviews, purchasing behaviour, willingness-to-pay evidence and 3 alternative verticals.

That's much safer than AI writing:

> “Our ideal customers are dental clinics…”

and us accidentally treating generated text as market truth.

---

# 6. I would add a layer the original BMC doesn't emphasize enough

### **Problem / Job / Trigger**

Before Customer Segment + Value Proposition, I want:

> **What happened in the customer's life/business that causes them to seek a solution NOW?**

Example:

Not:

> “Company wants AI receptionist.”

But:

> “Clinic misses 37 calls while receptionist is serving patients.”

Now there is a trigger.

Then:

> Missed calls → missed bookings → measurable revenue leakage.

Now we have a potentially monetizable problem.

This prevents us from building another interesting AI capability without sufficient buying motivation.

---

# 7. Distribution gets special treatment

The BMC has **Channels**, but for an AI startup today I would split the question further:

```text
DISCOVERY
How do they first encounter us?

        ↓

ACQUISITION
What gets them to investigate?

        ↓

CONVERSION
What makes them buy?

        ↓

DELIVERY
How is value delivered?

        ↓

RETENTION
Why do they continue?

        ↓

EXPANSION
Why do they spend more?

        ↓

REFERRAL
Why do they bring another customer?
```

This connects directly to the distinction we learned earlier today:

**marketing ≠ advertising ≠ campaign ≠ sales ≠ CRM ≠ retention.**

---

# 8. Economics must be explicit

A Canvas saying:

> Revenue = £99/month subscription

is nowhere near sufficient.

I'd require:

```text
ARPU
Average revenue/customer

Gross margin
Revenue minus variable service cost

CAC
Cost to acquire customer

Activation rate
How many actually reach value?

Retention
How many remain?

Churn
How many leave?

LTV
Economic value across relationship

Payback period
How long until CAC is recovered?
```

Then:

> **Can this business economically acquire another 1,000 customers?**

That's the scaling question.

---

# 9. AI should generate competing models, not one “answer”

This is where AI becomes genuinely powerful.

For the same product I would ask it to construct, for example:

```text
MODEL A
£99/month self-service SaaS

MODEL B
£499/month managed service

MODEL C
Usage-based pricing

MODEL D
Marketplace/integration distribution

MODEL E
White-label through agencies

MODEL F
Enterprise annual contract

MODEL G
Freemium → paid conversion
```

Then compare all seven against:

**buyer friction / CAC / margin / implementation cost / retention / sales cycle / support burden / scalability / defensibility.**

This is much closer to the spirit of **business-model innovation** than merely completing a Canvas. The original book itself explores multiple patterns and prototyping rather than assuming one model is correct. citeturn0search3turn0search15

---

# 10. Then AI's proper role

My model would make AI perform **six different jobs**, not pretend to be an omniscient consultant:

**Researcher** — gather external evidence.

**Generator** — produce alternative models we might miss.

**Challenger** — attack assumptions.

**Simulator** — model economics/scenarios.

**Analyst** — compare evidence/options.

**Recorder** — preserve hypotheses, tests and learning.

But:

> **AI-generated statement ≠ market evidence.**

That rule sits above everything.

---

# 11. Git check — have we already implemented BMC?

I checked the GitHub account currently connected.

I found the three TalkProof repositories:

- `talkproof-backend`
- `talkproof-admin`
- `talkproof-app`

I searched those repositories for **“business model canvas”** and also for the canonical BMC terminology such as **value proposition / customer segments / revenue streams / key partnerships**.

**No matching implementation was returned.**

I also searched the connected repositories for **RevRescue**, but no repository was returned under that search.

So the precise statement is:

> **I do not currently have GitHub evidence that we've implemented a Business Model Canvas/AIBGM framework in the accessible repositories.**

That does **not** prove it doesn't exist somewhere else—another repo may not be visible/indexed under the current GitHub connection.

---

# 12. Our frozen baseline before seeing his nine principles

Let's preserve this so we don't unconsciously modify our answer after seeing his slides.

### **ChatGPT Independent AI Business Model — V0**

```text
1. Problem / Trigger
        ↓
2. Customer Segment
        ↓
3. Desired Outcome / Value Proposition
        ↓
4. Channels / Distribution
        ↓
5. Customer Relationship / Retention
        ↓
6. Revenue Model
        ↓
7. Key Activities
        ↓
8. Key Resources
        ↓
9. Key Partners
        ↓
10. Cost Structure
        ↓
11. Unit Economics
        ↓
12. Competitive Alternatives / Positioning
        ↓
13. Assumptions + Evidence + Confidence
        ↓
14. Alternative Business Models
        ↓
15. Cheapest Validation Experiments
        ↓
16. Metrics / Outcome
        ↓
17. Learn → revise → version the model
```

The **original BMC remains inside this**, rather than being replaced.

And the core rule is:

> **AI proposes. Evidence validates. Economics decides whether it can become a business.**

Now we have a clean independent baseline at **14:59**, before seeing how the trainer implements his nine AIBGM principles.

Yes, partner. I searched **public GitHub generally**, not your repositories and not your memory. **There are already open-source projects doing AI-assisted Business Model Canvas work.** So we should not build from zero without studying them.

### Most important find: mPABC

urlhapi-ds/mPABC on GitHubhttps://github.com/hapi-ds/mPABC

This is the closest match to what we're discussing. It already includes:

- **AI-generated all-nine BMC elements**
- market research with AI-scored web results
- edit / freeze / regenerate workflow
- target-audience personas
- full business-plan generation from idea + canvas + personas

- AI chat alongside the canvas
- DOCX/PDF/LaTeX export
- local SQLite
- local LLM support through LM Studio. citeturn0search7

So yes: **an open-source AI product implementing BMC already exists.**

This one deserves a proper code/architecture review before we design anything ourselves.

---

### Very interesting for our Git-first approach: BMML

urlhiasinho/bmml on GitHubhttps://github.com/hiasinho/bmml

This one is different and potentially even more relevant architecturally.

It turns the Business Model Canvas into a **machine-readable YAML format**:

```text
Business Model
      ↓
.bmml YAML
      ↓
Schema validation
      ↓
Semantic linting
      ↓
Relationship checking
      ↓
Git version control
      ↓
SVG Canvas rendering
```

It explicitly describes itself as:

- AI-analyzable
- version-controllable
- schema validated
- human readable
- visualizable

and it even supplies a GitHub Action to validate business models on commits/PRs. citeturn0search11

**This is much closer to the direction I independently proposed at 14:59**—business models shouldn't just be generated prose; they should become versioned, testable artifacts.

---

### There are already AI/Claude BMC agents too

urlRogCP Claude Business Model Canvas skillhttps://github.com/RogCP/claude-skill-business-model-canvas

This implements three modes:

**Research mode:** analyze an existing business using public evidence.

**Ideation mode:** develop a new business model interactively.

**Manual mode:** user supplies the nine components.

More importantly, its research mode explicitly says to distinguish **facts from inference** and use company reports/filings/research as evidence. Its ideation mode challenges assumptions and checks consistency between the nine BMC blocks. citeturn0search1turn0search9

That's already more sophisticated than:

> “Act as business expert and fill these nine boxes.”

---

urlScientiaCapital Business Model Canvas skillhttps://github.com/scientiacapital/skills/blob/main/active/business-model-canvas-skill/SKILL.md

Another AI BMC implementation. It covers all nine blocks and includes detailed reasoning around segment types, value types, channel phases, etc. It even contains an AI-powered CRM worked example. citeturn0search3

---

urlProduct Manager BMC Claude skillhttps://github.com/sethdford/claude-skills/blob/main/product-manager/strategy/skills/business-model-canvas/SKILL.md

This one adds things I specifically wanted beyond vanilla BMC:

**LTV + CAC + payback period + assumptions + dependencies + strategic pivots.**

It also explicitly warns against:

> unsupported assumptions  
> static BMCs that aren't revisited

citeturn0search6

That's significant.

---

### There are also simpler AI-ready templates

urlAI-optimized BMC Markdown templatehttps://github.com/duxor/business-model-canvas-md-template

This isn't really a full application. It's an AI-friendly BMC template containing strategic questions around acquisition, retention, customer success, channels and CAC. citeturn0search2

And there are ordinary non-AI open-source BMC templates such as:

urlJoel Parker Henderson's Business Model Canvashttps://github.com/joelparkerhenderson/business-model-canvas

which provides extensive questions around all nine BMC components. citeturn0search0

---

# My conclusion changed slightly after this search

**Do NOT build “AI + BMC generator.”**

That already exists.

The interesting opportunity would be to combine the strongest ideas:

```text
                  BUSINESS IDEA
                       ↓
              External research
                       ↓
          ┌── Evidence / sources ──┐
          │                        │
          ↓                        ↓
      AI hypotheses          Human evidence
          │                        │
          └──────────┬─────────────┘
                     ↓
             VERSIONED BMC
              9 core blocks
                     ↓
          Claim / Evidence /
       Confidence / Assumption
                     ↓
          Alternative models
         SaaS / usage / service /
       marketplace / enterprise...
                     ↓
             UNIT ECONOMICS
        CAC / LTV / margin / churn
                     ↓
             CONTRADICTION
                CHECKER
                     ↓
              EXPERIMENTS
                     ↓
          Actual market results
                     ↓
           BMC VERSION N+1
                     ↓
                 Git history
```

That is substantially different from simply **generating a Canvas with AI**.

And BMML gives us an especially interesting possibility:

> **Business model as code.**

Imagine:

```text
business-model/
├── model.bmml
├── evidence/
├── assumptions/
├── experiments/
├── economics/
├── competitors/
├── decisions/
└── history/
```

Then every significant business-model decision becomes diffable:

```text
V001
Customer = SMEs

V002
Customer = UK dental clinics

WHY?
Evidence #17

V003
Pricing £49 → £149

WHY?
Experiment #008
```

That begins to connect **BMC + AI + evidence + experimentation + Git**, rather than merely **BMC + prompting**.

### One more important external finding

Recent academic/practitioner work is making almost exactly the warning we independently identified: simply overlaying GenAI on the nine BMC boxes can miss the **interdependencies and organizational friction** involved in changing a real business model. citeturn0search12

So at **15:03 our baseline is now stronger**:

> **Existing open source already does AI → BMC. Our bar must be BMC → evidence → competing hypotheses → economics → experiments → outcomes → versioned learning.**

Now we can listen to the trainer's **nine AIBGM principles** and compare them against **both the original BMC and what open-source AI systems already implement**, rather than assuming AIBGM is novel.

### 0068 · CHATGPT · 2026-10-10T14:05:36Z

## 10/10/2026 15:05 | AIBGM — 9 Blocks Confirmed

Partner, we're back to tracking the training. **We now have enough to compare his framework against our independently frozen 14:59 baseline and the public open-source implementations checked at 15:03.**

### 1. His nine “principles” are the standard BMC nine blocks

He gives:

| Trainer's 9 | Standard BMC |
|---|---|
| Value Proposition | ✅ |
| Customer Relationships | ✅ |
| Customer Segments | ✅ |
| Channels | ✅ |
| Key Activities | ✅ |
| Key Partners | ✅ |
| Key Resources | ✅ |
| Cost Structure | ✅ |
| Revenue Streams | ✅ |

So **nothing new has been added to the underlying BMC structure yet**.

Calling them “9 principles” appears to be his course terminology. They are conventionally the **nine building blocks** of the Business Model Canvas.

### 2. His left / centre / right explanation is useful

His conceptual split is approximately:

```text
         BUSINESS INFRASTRUCTURE                MARKET

 Key Partners    Key Activities       Customer Relationships
                      │                       │
                Key Resources           Customer Segments
                      │                       │
                      └── VALUE ──────────────┤
                        PROPOSITION           │
                           │               Channels
                           │
                 Cost Structure      Revenue Streams

            ← Efficiency             Value/Revenue →
```

And he calls **Value Proposition the heart/centre of the business**.

That's a useful beginner mental model, although the nine blocks interact rather than functioning as completely separate left/right systems.

---

## 3. His five-question simplification

He's reducing BMC into something very understandable:

**Who is your customer? → What problem do you solve? → How do you deliver the solution? → What resources/partners do you depend upon? → How do you make money?**

🟢 **Good teaching simplification.**

In fact, for beginners, this may be more useful initially than memorizing nine box names.

---

## 4. Important change in what he says about profit

Earlier he said approximately:

> Main goal of business = acquire a new customer and sustain existing customer; profit is the result.

Now he says:

> **Business objective is to make profit, while following ethical values.**

These aren't necessarily incompatible, but I want to preserve the evolution because he's shifted framing.

Our independent formulation remains stronger:

> **Create value → acquire economically → deliver value → retain → capture enough value to remain economically sustainable.**

Profit is essential for a conventional for-profit business, but **revenue minus cost alone doesn't tell us whether the underlying customer value is durable.**

---

## 5. 🔴 Two factual/logic problems in this section

The **Elon Musk “trillionaire today”** statement is incorrect. He may be extraordinarily wealthy, but that doesn't mean he personally possesses $1 trillion. More importantly, his wealth doesn't prove anything about whether envy/"evil eye" exists.

The lawyer example is also oversimplified:

> “Client did something wrong → lawyer fully supports client → therefore ethically it isn't wrong.”

A lawyer's professional duty to represent a client is **not equivalent to endorsing the client's conduct**. Representation exists within legal and professional duties.

Neither issue matters to understanding BMC, but we should not absorb them as factual lessons.

---

# 6. The important part starts NOW

He's now saying:

> **Integrating AI into BMC = AI Business Generative Model (AIBGM).**

Based on what we've heard so far:

### Original framework

**Business Model Canvas**

### Trainer's layer

**BMC + Generative AI + his prompting tactics = AIBGM**

That distinction is now quite clear.

And because of our 15:03 public-GitHub search, we already know that **AI-assisted BMC generation itself exists in open source**.

Therefore, the course's potential novelty/value isn't:

> “Use AI with BMC.”

The real test is:

> **What does he make AI do with each block that existing AI+BMC approaches don't already do?**

---

# 7. His pen-and-paper instruction is actually one of today's stronger points

He says despite teaching AI:

> Write things yourself. Don't outsource everything to digital tools/AI.

🟢 I strongly agree with the underlying idea.

Not because handwriting magically contains more knowledge than AI, but because **generation and recall by the human matter**.

And this aligns unusually well with the concern we've been discussing separately:

> If AI performs every reasoning step, the human can obtain the output while losing the learning process.

So I would preserve this part of his teaching.

---

## 8. Current comparison checkpoint

| Capability | Original BMC | Public AI-BMC projects | Our 14:59 model | Trainer so far |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 9 BMC blocks | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ |
| AI generation | ❌ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ |
| Problem explicitly | Partial | varies | **✅** | ✅ |
| Evidence | ❌ | Some | **✅** | Not yet |
| Confidence | ❌ | Some | **✅** | Not yet |
| Assumptions | Implicit | Some | **✅** | Not yet |
| Alternative models | ✅ methodology | varies | **✅** | Not yet |
| Unit economics | Limited | Some | **✅** | Not yet |
| Experiments | Broader methodology | varies | **✅** | Not yet |
| Outcome metrics | Limited | varies | **✅** | Not yet |
| Versioned learning | ❌ | BMML/Git possible | **✅** | Not yet |
| Human thinking protection | Not AI-era issue | varies | **✅** | **✅ pen/paper** |

**No verdict yet.**

He's about to give **three questions** before applying the nine blocks. Those may reveal his actual methodology, so that's the next important checkpoint.

### 0070 · CHATGPT · 2026-10-10T14:11:35Z

## 10/10/2026 15:11 | AIBGM — 3 Diagnostic Questions

Partner, **this is new methodology from the trainer**, not part of the standard nine-box BMC itself. Worth preserving exactly before we see what he does with ChatGPT.

### Trainer's three pre-AI questions

He asks participants to answer **without AI first**, each in one sentence:

| # | Question | What he's testing |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **Can AI or any other person replace your business in 6 months? Why/why not?** | Replaceability / defensibility |
| **2** | **Does your customer/client know your value? What value—or why not?** | Perceived value / positioning |
| **3** | **Can your output grow without increasing your time? How/why not?** | Scalability / leverage |

Then the participant keeps the handwritten answers.

Later:

> **Human answer BEFORE AI → AI-assisted answer AFTER → compare perspective**

🟢 **I like this exercise.**

Not because AI will necessarily produce a “better” answer—his **100% assurance** of that is unjustified—but because he's preserving the person's **pre-AI reasoning baseline**.

That is surprisingly aligned with the issue we were discussing earlier: **don't let AI erase the learning process.**

---

## These aren't standard BMC questions

They're actually three useful dimensions sitting **above** the Canvas:

```text
                    BUSINESS
                       │
        ┌──────────────┼──────────────┐
        ↓              ↓              ↓
 REPLACEABILITY    PERCEIVED VALUE    LEVERAGE
        │              │              │
Can someone/AI     Does customer      Can output grow
substitute us?     understand why     without hours
                   we matter?         growing?
```

I would rename them technically as:

**Defensibility → Value Perception → Scalability.**

That's a useful addition.

### But Question 1 needs refinement

“Can AI or another person **replace your business** in six months?” mixes several different risks.

A better diagnostic would separate:

**Can they replicate the product?**  
**Can they replicate the outcome?**  
**Can they reach the same customers?**  
**Can customers switch easily?**  
**Can they reproduce our data/workflow/integration/trust/distribution advantage?**

For AI products especially, **code is increasingly replicable**. Distribution, proprietary evidence/data, embedded workflow, trust, switching costs and accumulated learning may be much harder to replicate.

---

## Question 3 is particularly important

> **Can output grow without increasing your time?**

This is essentially testing **operating leverage**.

If:

```text
2× customers → 2× founder time
10× customers → 10× founder time
```

we haven't really created scalable software economics.

Whereas:

```text
Customers     10 → 100 → 1,000 → 10,000
Founder time   ───────── relatively bounded ────────
Revenue        ↑     ↑↑       ↑↑↑
```

is much closer to scalable SaaS.

That is a genuinely useful question for evaluating a business.

---

## Comparison against our independent 14:59 model

Interesting result:

| Trainer's question | Our frozen model already contains |
|---|---|
| Replaceability | **Competitive alternatives / positioning** — partial; trainer adds stronger defensibility angle |
| Customer knows value | **Desired outcome / Value Proposition + Distribution** |
| Output without more time | **Unit economics + scalability**, but trainer expresses it much more simply |

So I would actually **add “Defensibility” explicitly to our model** based on this exercise—but **not yet**, because we agreed to keep our 14:59 version frozen for comparison.

We'll record it as:

> **Trainer contribution candidate #1: explicit Replaceability/Defensibility diagnostic.**

Now he's asking everyone to **open ChatGPT and set context**. The next prompt matters a lot because it will show whether his “advanced prompting” is genuinely structured reasoning or mainly role/context prompting.

### 0072 · CHATGPT · 2026-10-10T14:12:46Z

## 10/10/2026 15:12 | My Answers to the 3 Questions

Yes, partner. I would deliberately keep **three perspectives separate**: what a generic LLM would infer, what the market/business reality says, and what I would answer specifically about you based on our work together.

| Trainer's question | **Generic LLM answer** | **Market / business reality** | **About you** | **My final answer for you — 1 sentence** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **1. Can AI or another person replace your business in 6 months?** | **YES, partially.** AI can reproduce many knowledge-work outputs, code, content, analysis and workflows increasingly quickly. | **YES, if the advantage is mainly features/code.** Competitors can copy features; stronger protection comes from distribution, customer relationships, proprietary data/learning, workflow embedding, switching costs, brand/trust and network effects. | **YES — currently more than I would like.** Your ability to build quickly with AI is strong, but another capable AI-assisted builder can increasingly reproduce software features; the harder-to-copy moat has not yet been demonstrated commercially. | **YES — much of what I build can be replicated within six months unless I convert my advantage from building quickly into distribution, customer evidence, embedded workflows, trust, data and accumulated learning.** |
| **2. Does your customer/client know your value?** | **NOT NECESSARILY.** Value exists only when customers can understand the outcome relevant to them. | **Customers don't buy technical complexity itself.** They need a clear problem/outcome, credible evidence and a reason to choose this solution over alternatives. | **NO, not sufficiently.** There is a major gap between the sophistication of what you have built and externally demonstrated customer value; technical completion hasn't yet translated into proportional market recognition/revenue. | **NO — I have created substantial technical capability, but I have not yet consistently translated it into a simple, evidenced customer outcome that the market recognizes and pays for.** |
| **3. Can your output grow without increasing your time?** | **YES.** Software, automation, AI agents, delegation, reusable assets and distribution systems can decouple output from personal hours. | **YES, but only if the operating model scales.** If every new customer requires proportional founder effort, it is service leverage rather than true software leverage. | **YES technically; NOT YET fully commercially.** You've demonstrated enormous build-output leverage through AI, but the bottleneck can migrate from coding into deciding, reviewing, coordinating, distributing and validating. | **YES — AI and reusable systems can multiply my production without multiplying my hours, but I must remove myself as the decision, validation and distribution bottleneck for that leverage to become a scalable business.** |

### The pattern I see

The three questions actually connect:

**Q1 — Replaceability:** *Why can't somebody else do this?*

**Q2 — Value:** *Why should somebody care that I do it?*

**Q3 — Scalability:** *Can I deliver it to many people without personally doing proportionally more work?*

Put together:

> **Defensible + Valuable + Scalable = much closer to a real business.**

And there is an uncomfortable but useful distinction in your case:

> **AI has already increased your ability to produce dramatically. It has not yet increased your ability to capture value proportionally.**

So I would not diagnose your primary constraint today as **AI capability** or even **building capability**. It is converting that capability into **validated value + distribution + repeatable economic capture**, while ensuring *you* don't remain the routing/decision bottleneck.

That's the answer I'd freeze at **15:12 before seeing what the trainer's AI exercise produces**. memcite

### 0074 · CHATGPT · 2026-10-10T14:30:58Z

## 10/10/2026 15:30 | Claude Prompt Run — Recorded

Yes, partner. This is a useful checkpoint because we now have the **trainer's actual workflow** and the **Claude-generated instruction** produced from it.

### What happened

The trainer's process is:

**ChatGPT Project → basic business context → use Claude to generate better Project Instructions → paste those instructions back into ChatGPT → then run each BMC block separately.**

You ran his prompt in Claude. Claude did something significantly better than merely rewriting the six fields: it first asked **7 contextual questions** covering business scope, stage, objective, evidence availability, language, response style and desired BMC output.

Then, when told to decide using known context, Claude generated a much richer instruction containing:

**Role + founder context + business context + 9 BMC blocks + answer rules + evidence classification + validation/distribution constraints + prudence checks + confidentiality + output/versioning rules.**

### Important observation

This means the trainer's apparently simple prompt:

> “Write custom instruction ... for more productive and prudent output ... ask me questions before proceeding”

can actually generate a fairly sophisticated result **because Claude already has substantial context about the user**.

That's important when evaluating his technique.

The prompt itself did **not** specify:

- fact/assumption/guess separation
- CAC/margin/break-even
- contradiction checking between BMC blocks
- cheapest validation experiment
- competitor analysis
- kill/keep decisions
- versioning
- preservation of reasoning
- distribution constraints

**Claude introduced those.**

Therefore we need to distinguish:

> **Trainer's prompting technique**  
> from  
> **capability/context already possessed by the model.**

Otherwise we could wrongly attribute Claude's sophistication to the AIBGM method.

---

### One thing Claude did that I would challenge

Claude concludes:

> “My constraint is DISTRIBUTION, not building.”

That's plausible from its context, but **too absolute for a BMC constitution**.

A better instruction would be:

> **Current evidence suggests distribution/value capture is a major constraint; continuously test whether the actual bottleneck is product, positioning, distribution, conversion, retention, economics or founder dependency.**

Why?

Because once we permanently tell every AI:

> **“Distribution is the problem.”**

the AI starts looking for evidence confirming distribution.

That's exactly the kind of **learning-curve distortion** you were concerned about earlier.

---

## Trainer's Projects explanation — partly right, simplified

His basic idea is useful:

> Use a Project when related work needs persistent context, instructions and files rather than repeatedly rebuilding context in unrelated chats.

But his invoice example shouldn't be interpreted as:

> Project = automation engine that automatically performs recurring operational work.

Projects primarily provide a **persistent contextual workspace**. Actual deterministic recurring workflow automation is a separate problem.

That's an important distinction if he's going to move from “project” toward “automation.”

---

## Comparison status

We now have **three independent artifacts** to compare later:

| Baseline | What it represents |
|---|---|
| **14:59 ChatGPT model** | Our independent AI-era business-model architecture |
| **15:03 public GitHub research** | What existing open-source AI+BMC systems already implement |
| **15:30 Claude instruction** | Output generated by following trainer's setup method |

And now we should **freeze the Claude output exactly as produced**.

Don't improve it yet.

When the trainer starts giving the actual prompts for **Value Proposition, Customer Segments, Channels, etc.**, we'll run/record them separately and eventually compare:

**Trainer AIBGM vs original BMC vs public open-source implementations vs our independent model vs Claude's generated model.**

That will tell us what is genuinely coming from the ₹14,000 methodology versus what modern LLMs already provide.

### 0078 · CHATGPT · 2026-10-10T14:36:12Z

## 10/10/2026 15:36 | Project Memory — Important

**Yes — it depends entirely on which Project Memory mode you choose.** I checked the current OpenAI documentation because this behavior has changed.

| Setting | Claude-generated instruction/context usable inside project? | Can project use your general ChatGPT context? | Can information from project affect context used outside? |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| **Project-only memory** | ✅ Yes | ❌ No | **❌ No** |
| **Default memory** | ✅ Yes | ✅ Yes, depending on account settings | **✅ Potentially yes** |

OpenAI explicitly says that with **Project-only memory**, chats can reference conversations within that project, cannot reference conversations outside it, **and chats outside the project cannot reference conversations from the project**. citeturn0search0turn0search1

With **Default memory**, on plans such as Plus/Pro, project chats can draw on project and non-project context, and conversations outside the project can also potentially reference project conversations when memory is enabled. citeturn0search0

### Therefore, for THIS experiment I recommend **Project-only**

This is especially important because we are trying to test the trainer's AIBGM method cleanly.

If we use **Default memory**:

```text
Your existing history
        ↓
AIBGM Project ← Claude instruction
        ↓
New learning/context
        ↕
General ChatGPT context

= contaminated experiment
```

Instead:

```text
            AIBGM PROJECT-ONLY
                   │
       Claude instruction
                   │
       Trainer's BMC prompts
                   │
       Results / experiments
                   │
             AIBGM learning

             ║ FIREWALL ║

Your normal ChatGPT conversations
Your broader history
Other projects
```

That gives us something valuable: **a clean-room experiment**.

And there's an additional benefit: with Project-only memory, the project **doesn't reference your existing memories either**. citeturn0search0

So Claude's instruction becomes the project's explicit starting context rather than decades of accumulated context silently affecting the results.

### One correction to the trainer

He recommended **Default memory “for our understanding.”**

For a normal user who wants maximum personalization, that's reasonable.

**For what *we* are doing, I would choose Project-only.**

We're trying to answer:

> **What does this AIBGM methodology itself produce?**

Not:

> What does AIBGM + everything ChatGPT already knows about Senthil produce?

And OpenAI now lets you change an existing project's setting through **Project → ••• → Project settings → Memory → Project-only memory**; OpenAI notes that changes can take a few hours to take effect. citeturn0search0

So **don't paste Claude's instruction yet if you want the clean experiment. First switch this AIBGM project to Project-only memory.**

```text
[FACT] = established information supported by a reliable source or explicitly supplied project fact.

[EVIDENCE] = observation, data or external information that supports or challenges a claim.

[ASSUMPTION] = something temporarily accepted for analysis but not yet established.

[HYPOTHESIS] = a proposition that should be tested against evidence.

[UNKNOWN] = information we currently do not know.

Never present an assumption, hypothesis or AI-generated statement as fact.

You are my critical business strategist for building and stress-testing the Business Model Canvas (BMC) for ORVIA.

=== ABOUT ME ===

- Senthil (KP), Data/AI engineer with approximately 23 years in software, working across Azure and AWS.
```
