## ORVIA SOM — Next 12 Months

**Verdict: plan around 25 paying customers, treat 50 as best-case.** A realistic provisional SOM is therefore **£72k–£143k exit ARR**, assuming the current £239/month model.

Important: [UNKNOWN] you haven't specified an actual monthly acquisition budget or 12-month revenue target, so I won't invent them. The numbers below are **capacity scenarios**, not forecasts.

| **12-month outcome**         | **Worst/base** | **Best case** |
| ---------------------------- | -------------: | ------------: |
| Paying customers at year-end |         **25** |        **50** |
| Price                        |        £239/mo |       £239/mo |
| Exit MRR                     |     **£5,975** |   **£11,950** |
| Exit ARR                     |    **£71,700** |  **£143,400** |
| SAM captured                 |          0.14% |         0.28% |
| TAM captured                 |         0.028% |        0.056% |

### Why I wouldn't model hundreds yet

[FACT] Founder capacity is currently the principal team resource available to this model.

[EVIDENCE] Technical build capability is strong, but sufficient repeatable paying-customer acquisition has not yet been demonstrated.

[HYPOTHESIS] **25 customers** is achievable only if ORVIA makes onboarding and implementation substantially reusable rather than providing bespoke automation to each customer.

**50 customers** requires stronger leverage: productised onboarding, reusable integrations/workflows, low-touch support and a repeatable non-founder-led acquisition channel.

That moves ORVIA toward **scalability Level 3–4**, rather than simply using AI to make founder work faster.

### Revenue reality

At 25 customers, ORVIA reaches **£5,975 MRR**.

At 50, **£11,950 MRR**.

But exit ARR isn't the same as first-year collected revenue because customers arrive progressively. For example, assuming roughly linear acquisition and ignoring churn, approximate first-year subscription revenue would be only **\~£36k** at the 25-customer exit case and **\~£72k** at 50 customers.

So I would set the provisional objective as:

> **Prove that ORVIA can reach 25 retained customers at \~£239/month through a repeatable acquisition and onboarding system—not maximise theoretical market share.**

### Biggest uncertainty

**CAC.** Without acquisition budget + traffic → trial → paid conversion evidence, even **25 customers remains a [HYPOTHESIS]**, not a credible forecast.

**One next step:** give me your **maximum monthly acquisition budget and desired monthly revenue after 12 months**; I'll reverse-engineer the required traffic, conversion rate, CAC, churn and customer count and tell you whether the target is economically credible.

### 0108 · CHATGPT · 2026-10-10T15:23:51Z

## 10/10/2026 16:23 | SOM + Day 1 Customer Segment Close

Partner, this closes today's **Customer Segments** principle. And the SOM result exposes something important: **the AI did not really calculate a new SOM—it propagated assumptions from our previous turns.**

### Trainer's final prompt

> **Estimate my Serviceable Obtainable Market (SOM) for the next 12 months, factoring in my team size, monthly budget, and revenue target. Give me a realistic best-case and worst-case number.**

Trainer's own example apparently concludes he could reach around **₹16.8 lakh/month** and provides calculations, team constraints, a 7-day validation test, recommendations and follow-up questions.

The good part is that he's now trying to connect:

**Market → resources → acquisition capacity → revenue target → obtainable market.**

That's much more useful than stopping at TAM.

---

## What happened in our clean-room Project

ChatGPT correctly refused to pretend it knew information we hadn't supplied:

> **“You haven't specified an actual monthly acquisition budget or 12-month revenue target, so I won't invent them.”**

🟢 **This is exactly the behaviour we wanted from our Project Instructions.**

But then it retained the previous scenario:

**25 customers → £5,975 MRR → £71,700 exit ARR**

**50 customers → £11,950 MRR → £143,400 exit ARR**

And it explicitly labels those as **capacity scenarios, not forecasts**.

### But 25 and 50 still aren't evidence

This is important.

The apparent progression is:

**TAM ~90,000**  
↓ assumed 20%  
**SAM ~18,000**  
↓ assumed achievable capacity  
**SOM 25–50**

But we still don't know why the obtainable number should be **25 rather than 7, 60 or 150**.

We lack:

**budget → traffic/discovery → qualified prospects → trial → paid conversion → churn → retained customers**

and:

**implementation hours/customer → support hours/customer → maximum simultaneous customers.**

Therefore the current SOM is really:

> **12-month planning scenario: 25–50 retained customers**

rather than a defensible market forecast.

---

## One thing the Project did particularly well

It distinguishes:

### Exit ARR ≠ first-year revenue

50 customers at £239/month gives:

**£11,950 exit MRR → £143,400 annualised run rate**

but that doesn't mean we collected £143,400 during Year 1.

If customers arrive progressively, actual first-year subscription revenue could be much lower.

That distinction is **commercially important** and easy to miss in AI-generated business plans.

---

# What Day 1 actually taught

The trainer says today covered only **one of the nine BMC principles: Customer Segments**.

But inside that one block he actually traversed quite a lot:

**Customer Segments**

→ Demographic / Geographic / Psychographic / Behavioral

→ **STP**
Segmentation → Targeting → Positioning

→ **ICP**

→ **Buyer Persona**

→ **Customer Journey Mapping**

→ **TAM**

→ **SAM**

→ **SOM**

So although much of the theory is established marketing/business material, the **sequence is sensible**.

It progressively moves:

> **Who might buy? → Who should I focus on? → Which organisation? → Which person? → How do they buy? → How big is the opportunity? → What can I realistically capture?**

That's a coherent learning path.

---

## Day 1 scorecard so far

| Area | Assessment |
|---|---|
| BMC introduction | 🟡 Standard |
| 9 BMC blocks | 🟡 Standard |
| Customer segmentation | 🟡 Standard |
| STP | 🟡 Standard, useful simplification |
| ICP vs Buyer Persona | 🟢 Useful |
| Customer Journey prompt | 🟢 Good practical prompt |
| TAM/SAM/SOM | 🟢 Useful, but high assumption risk |
| Evidence discipline | 🔴 Not strong enough from trainer alone |
| AI-generated customer quotes | ⚠️ Could easily be mistaken for evidence |
| Prompt design | 🟢 Practical |
| Novel business theory | 🔴 None demonstrated yet |
| Novel AI methodology | 🟡 Not yet clearly demonstrated |
| Applying AI to established frameworks | 🟢 This is the actual value so far |

I would **not dismiss today's class as merely beginner material**. But I also wouldn't call what we've seen “advanced AI” yet.

The strongest trainer value so far is **orchestration**: taking established business frameworks and giving participants a structured sequence of prompts that turns vague business thinking into progressively narrower hypotheses.

---

## And our experiment has already proved something useful

Compare what the trainer celebrates—very rich ChatGPT responses—with what happened in our isolated Project.

His prompt supplied the **question structure**.

Our Project Instructions supplied much of the:

**skepticism → evidence labels → assumptions → alternative explanations → economic reasoning → defensibility → scalability → kill criteria.**

That's precisely why our outputs look unusually strong.

### Our current ORVIA hypothesis — freeze, don't believe yet

Today's exercise has converged on:

> **UK property-maintenance / multi-trade businesses, roughly 5–30 employees**

with:

> **Missed enquiry → tracked response → booked job**

and possible positioning around:

> **Making the customer's existing people and systems operate as one flow rather than selling another AI tool.**

Status:

**[HYPOTHESIS] — interesting enough to validate, nowhere near enough evidence to build heavily.**

And I agree with your choice to **not validate it yet**. We should finish observing the trainer's complete methodology first. Otherwise we contaminate the clean experiment by turning our own analysis into the subsequent context.

One final trainer claim from the closing Q&A should also remain separate: AI predicting the **next Tamil Nadu CM** is not something that can be assured. AI can analyse polling, electoral history, alliances, public data and scenarios; that does not turn an uncertain future election into a knowable fact.

**Day 1 Customer Segment checkpoint captured.**

## 10/10/2026 16:25 | Day 1 Class Closed

Yes, partner. **Day 1 has formally ended.** The trainer confirms that despite the amount covered, he considers today only **one BMC category: Customer Segments**.

### Trainer's closing message

Today's work should be reviewed before tomorrow because subsequent BMC blocks will build on this context. He says the remaining principles will be more exciting and that later he will also cover **step-by-step implementation**, not merely idea generation.

Two homework items carry forward:

1. **Tea Shop dilemma** — determine which shop is “best” / the hidden mechanism. He still has **not revealed his answer**.
2. **Review today's generated business work** so tomorrow's prompts have stronger context.

Recording is promised **by tomorrow morning at the latest**, plus some additional material separately.

### Day 1 state we should freeze

Trainer's path was essentially:

**BMC**
→ Customer Segments  
→ segmentation types  
→ STP  
→ ICP  
→ Buyer Persona  
→ Customer Journey Mapping  
→ TAM  
→ SAM  
→ SOM

And we now have two parallel records:

**Trainer layer:** what he actually taught, prompts, terminology and claims.

**Our independent layer:** corrections, evidence quality, what came from his prompt vs our Project Instructions, and what remains hypothesis.

That's important because tomorrow we should **continue the same experiment rather than rewrite today's conclusions based on tomorrow's teaching.**

### Tea Shop remains deliberately unresolved

Our checkpoint remains:

> **The puzzle as originally stated is underdetermined.**

His later clues suggest some mechanism involving **competition/comparison/co-location/interdependence/positioning**, but we should **not manufacture his answer tonight**.

Tomorrow, when he reveals it, we can compare:

**our original answer → our later hypotheses → participant answers → trainer's actual answer → whether his answer logically follows from the original problem.**

That will be a particularly useful test of the course's claim about “human originality.”

### Most important Day 1 learning for *our* experiment

The class hasn't yet demonstrated novel business theory. Its useful contribution so far is more practical:

> **Established business frameworks + ordered prompting + persistent business context = progressively more specific business hypotheses.**

But our clean-room experiment revealed the other half:

> **Better prompting can produce much better-looking answers without producing better evidence.**

That's probably the single most important distinction to carry into Day 2.
